to unite Lithuania and Ruthenia under his rule and to receive a royal crown from the Holy See.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991242–243_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991242–243-141">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> According to the chronicle of <a href="/wiki/John_of_Posilge" class="mw-redirect" title="John of Posilge">John of Posilge</a>, who was an official of the Teutonic Order, Jadwiga sent a letter to Vytautas, reminding him to pay the annual tribute that Władysław-Jogaila had granted her as <a href="/wiki/Dower" title="Dower">dower</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201589_134-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201589-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991243–244_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991243–244-142">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Offended by Jadwiga's demand, Vytautas sought the opinion of the Lithuanian and Ruthenian lords who refused Jadwiga's claim to a tribute.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201589_134-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201589-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> On 12 October 1398, he signed a peace treaty with the Teutonic Knights, without referring to Władysław-Jogaila's right to confirm it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991242–243_141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991242–243-141">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Oscar Halecki says that Posilge's "sensational story" is either an invention based on gossip or a guess by the chronicler.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991244_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991244-143">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Pregnancy_and_death_.281399.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Pregnancy_and_death_(1399)">Pregnancy and death (1399)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Pregnancy and death (1399)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg/220px-Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg/330px-Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg/440px-Nagrobek_Jadwigi_Andegawe%C5%84skiej.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1935" data-file-height="1467" /></a><figcaption>Jadwiga's sarcophagus, <a href="/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral" title="Wawel Cathedral">Wawel Cathedral</a>, <a href="/wiki/Krak%C3%B3w" title="Kraków">Kraków</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Jadwiga was childless for over a decade, which, according to chronicles written in the Teutonic lands, caused conflicts between her and her husband.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991245_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991245-144">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> She became pregnant in late 1398 or early 1399.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991252_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991252-145">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Sigismund, King of Hungary, came to Kraków in early March to negotiate for a campaign to defend <a href="/wiki/Wallachia" title="Wallachia">Wallachia</a> against the Ottoman Turks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991252–253_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991252–253-146">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Vytautas, in order to bolster his authority over the Rus' principalities, decided to launch an expedition against <a href="/wiki/Timur" title="Timur">Timur</a>, who had subdued the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991256–257_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991256–257-147">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Jan_D%C5%82ugosz" title="Jan Długosz">Jan Długosz</a>'s chronicle, Jadwiga warned the Polish noblemen not to join Vytautas' campaign because it would end in failure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991256–257_147-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991256–257-147">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Halecki says that the great number of Polish knights who joined Vytautas's expedition proves that Długosz's report is not reliable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257-148">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the occasion of the expected birth to the royal couple, Jogaila's cousin <a href="/wiki/Vytautas" title="Vytautas">Vytautas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_of_Lithuania" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duke of Lithuania">Grand Duke of Lithuania</a>, sent expensive gifts, including a silver cradle, to the royal court on behalf of himself and his wife, <a href="/wiki/Anna,_Grand_Duchess_of_Lithuania" title="Anna, Grand Duchess of Lithuania">Anna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> The first horoscopes written for Jadwiga's and Jogaila's child predicted a son in mid-September 1398.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> However, a girl was delivered on 22 June 1399 at <a href="/wiki/Wawel_Castle" title="Wawel Castle">Wawel Castle</a>. Reports of the time stated that the child was born prematurely.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> According to the horoscope, she was actually born slightly late. However, a due date of 18 June would rule out the suspicion of pregnancy as early as mid-September.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The newborn princess was named Elizabeth Bonifacia (<a href="/wiki/Polish_language" title="Polish language">Polish</a>: <i lang="pl">Elżbieta Bonifacja</i>, <a href="/wiki/Lithuanian_language" title="Lithuanian language">Lithuanian</a>: <i lang="lt">Elżbieta Bonifacija</i>), after Jadwiga's mother and Pope Boniface IX who, in a letter of 5 May 1399, had agreed to be godfather under the condition that the infant be called Boniface or Bonifacia. She was baptised by <a href="/wiki/Piotr_Wysz_Radoli%C5%84ski" title="Piotr Wysz Radoliński">Piotr Wysz Radoliński</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Krak%C3%B3w" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Kraków">Bishop of Kraków</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> However, the infant died after only three weeks, on 13 July 1399.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga, too, was on her deathbed. <a href="/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_of_Skarbimierz" title="Stanisław of Skarbimierz">Stanisław of Skarbimierz</a> expressed hope that she would survive, describing her as the spiritual mother of the poor, weak, and ill of Poland.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999407–408_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999407–408-155">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> She advised her husband to marry <a href="/wiki/Anna_of_Cilli" title="Anna of Cilli">Anna of Cilli</a>, Casimir the Great's granddaughter<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991264–265_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991264–265-156">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>— which he did — and died on 17 July 1399, four days after her newborn daughter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257-148">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jadwiga and her daughter were buried together in <a href="/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral" title="Wawel Cathedral">Wawel Cathedral</a>, on 24 August 1399,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257_148-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991257-148">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> as stipulated in the Queen's last will. On 12 July 1949, 550 years later, their tomb was opened; nothing remained of the child's soft cartilage.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<p>The following family tree illustrates Jadwiga's connection to her notable relatives. Kings of Poland are colored blue.

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Achievements">Achievements</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Achievements">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg/220px-Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="277" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg/330px-Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg/440px-Jadwiga_by_Bacciarelli.jpg 2x" data-file-width="757" data-file-height="952" /></a><figcaption>Jadwiga as imagined by <a href="/wiki/Marcello_Bacciarelli" title="Marcello Bacciarelli">Marcello Bacciarelli</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Two leading historians, Oscar Halecki and S. Harrison Thomson, agree that Jadwiga was one of the greatest rulers of Poland, comparable to <a href="/wiki/Boles%C5%82aw_the_Brave" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolesław the Brave">Bolesław the Brave</a> and <a href="/wiki/Casimir_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Casimir the Great">Casimir the Great</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433-159">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Her marriage to Władysław-Jogaila enabled the <a href="/wiki/Union_of_Poland_and_Lithuania" class="mw-redirect" title="Union of Poland and Lithuania">union of Poland and Lithuania</a>, establishing a large state in East Central Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433-159">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga's decision to marry the 'elderly' Władysław-Jogaila instead of her beloved fiancé, William of Habsburg, has often been described as a sacrifice for her country in Polish historiography.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999434_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999434-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Her biographers emphasize Jadwiga's efforts to preserve the peace with the Teutonic Order, which enabled Poland to make preparations for a decisive war against the Knights.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga's childless death weakened Władysław-Jogaila's position, because his claim to Poland was based on their marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991263_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991263-161">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Six days after her funeral, Władysław-Jogaila left Poland for Ruthenia, stating that he was to return to Lithuania after his wife's death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The Polish lords sent their envoys to Lviv to open negotiations with him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The delegates took new oaths of loyalty to him, confirming his position as king.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> On the lords' demand, he agreed to marry Anna of Cilli.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrost201591_154-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrost201591-154">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Their wedding was celebrated on 29 January 1402.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991265_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991265-162">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jadwiga's cultural and charitable activities were of exceptional value.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> She established new hospitals, schools and churches, and restored older ones.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436_160-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga promoted the use of vernacular in church services, especially the singing of hymns in Polish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436_160-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> The Scriptures were translated into Polish on her order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436_160-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999436-160">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Casimir the Great had already in 1364 established the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Krak%C3%B3w" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Kraków">University of Kraków</a>, but it did not survive his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavies200580_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies200580-163">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Władysław-Jogaila and Jadwiga jointly asked Pope Boniface IX to sanction the establishment of a faculty of theology in Kraków.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991262_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991262-164">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> The pope granted their request on 11 January 1397.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991261–262_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991261–262-165">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga bought houses along a central street of Kraków for the university.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437_166-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> However, the faculty was only set up a year after Jadwiga's death: Władysław-Jogaila issued the charter for the reestablished university on 26 July 1400.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavies200580_163-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies200580-163">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991261–262_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991261–262-165">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437_166-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999437-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> In accordance with Jadwiga's last will, the restoration of the university was partially financed through the sale of her jewellery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991262_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991262-164">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Holiness">Holiness</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Holiness">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Saint Jadwiga of Poland</div></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">Beatified</a></th><td class="infobox-data">8 August 1986, Kraków, Poland by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data">8 June 1997, Kraków, Poland by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral" title="Wawel Cathedral">Wawel Cathedral</a>, Kraków, Poland</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">17 July</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Royal dress and shoes, apron full of roses</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Poland, queens, united Europe, students, mothers<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Oscar Halecki writes that Jadwiga transmitted to the nations of East Central Europe the "universal heritage of the <i><a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">respublica Christiana</a></i>, which in the West was then waning, but in East Central Europe started flourishing and blending with the pre-Renaissance world".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki199178_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki199178-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> She was closely related to the saintly 13th-century princesses, venerated in Hungary and Poland, including <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_of_Hungary" title="Elizabeth of Hungary">Elizabeth of Hungary</a> and her nieces, <a href="/wiki/Kinga_of_Poland" title="Kinga of Poland">Kinga</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yolanda_of_Poland" title="Yolanda of Poland">Yolanda</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Salomea_of_Poland" title="Salomea of Poland">Salomea of Poland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki199182,_90_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki199182,_90-168">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> She was born to a family famed for its religious zeal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEngel2001170–171_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEngel2001170–171-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433_159-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999433-159">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> She attended <a href="/wiki/Mass_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass (Catholic Church)">Mass</a> every day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGromada1999434_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGromada1999434-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In accordance with her family's tradition, Jadwiga was especially devoted to the Blessed <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Mary">Virgin Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991115_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991115-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> An inscription engraved on her request on a precious chalice, which was placed in the <a href="/wiki/Wawel_Cathedral" title="Wawel Cathedral">Wawel Cathedral</a>, asked Our Lady to place Poland under her protection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991115_170-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki1991115-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jadwiga was venerated in Poland soon after her death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_of_Skarbimierz" title="Stanisław of Skarbimierz">Stanisław of Skarbimierz</a> states that she had been "the most Christian queen" in his sermon composed for her funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408_171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Paul of Zator referred to the wax figures placed by her grave.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408_171-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Sermons written in the early 15th century emphasized that Jadwiga had been a representative of the traditional virtues of holy women, such as mercy and benevolence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408_171-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Jadwiga's contribution to the restoration of the University of Kraków was also mentioned by early 15th-century scholars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408_171-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrzezińska1999408-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Numerous legends about miracles were recounted to justify her sainthood. The two best-known are those of "Jadwiga's cross" and "Jadwiga's foot":
</p><p>Jadwiga often prayed before a large black <a href="/wiki/Crucifix" title="Crucifix">crucifix</a> hanging in the north aisle of Wawel Cathedral. During one of these prayers, the <a href="/wiki/Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ">Christ</a> on the cross is said to have spoken to her. The crucifix, "Saint Jadwiga's cross", is still there, with her relics beneath it. Because of this event, she is considered a medieval mystic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJasienica1988_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJasienica1988-172">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (September 2015)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> According to another legend, Jadwiga took a piece of jewellery from her foot and gave it to a poor stonemason who had begged for her help. When the king left, he noticed her footprint in the plaster floor of his workplace, even though the plaster had already hardened before her visit. The supposed footprint, known as "Jadwiga's foot", can still be seen in <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Visitation_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary,_Krakow" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of the Visitation of the Blessed Virgin Mary, Krakow">one of Kraków's churches</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In yet another legend, Jadwiga was taking part in a Corpus Christi Day procession when a coppersmith's son drowned by falling into a river. Jadwiga threw her mantle over the boy's body, and he regained life.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 8 June 1979 <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> prayed at her sarcophagus; and the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_Divine_Worship_and_the_Discipline_of_the_Sacraments" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for Divine Worship and the Discipline of the Sacraments">Congregation for Divine Worship and the Discipline of the Sacraments</a> officially affirmed her <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> on 8 August 1986. 
The Pope went on to canonize Jadwiga in Kraków on 8 June 1997.<sup id="cite_ref-Saint_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Saint-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Popular_culture">Popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><i>Hedvigis. Dziedziczka królestwa</i> (2021), a Polish historical novel about the early life and reign of Jadwiga by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Krzysztof_Konopka&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Krzysztof Konopka (page does not exist)">Krzysztof Konopka</a>, follows the story of Jadwiga, her sister Mary, and their mother.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Queen Jadwiga is the main character of the third season of Polish historical TV series <i><a href="/wiki/Korona_kr%C3%B3l%C3%B3w" title="Korona królów">Korona królów</a></i> (The Crown of the Kings). She is played by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Dagmara_Bryzek&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Dagmara Bryzek (page does not exist)">Dagmara Bryzek</a>. Child Jadwiga is played by Natalia Wolska and Amelia Zawadzka.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jadwiga appears as the leader of the Polish civilization in the turn-based strategy game <i><a href="/wiki/Civilization_VI" title="Civilization VI">Civilization VI</a></i>, specializing in religion and territorial expansion. She also features in <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Empires_II:_Definitive_Edition" title="Age of Empires II: Definitive Edition">Age of Empires II: Definitive Edition - Dawn of the Dukes</a> in a campaign of her own.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jadwiga is a playable character in the Mobile/PC Game <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Rise_of_Kingdoms&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Rise of Kingdoms (page does not exist)">Rise of Kingdoms</a></i>.
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jadwiga_of_Poland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Jadwiga was officially crowned as "King of Poland" — <i>Hedvig Rex Poloniæ</i>, not <i>Hedvig Regina Poloniæ</i>. Polish law had no provision for a female ruler (<a href="/wiki/Queen_regnant" title="Queen regnant">queen regnant</a>), but did not specify that the monarch had to be male.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-9"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-9">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Jadwiga's name was first recorded in instructions to Hungarian envoys to France on 17 April 1384.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki199156_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki199156-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> If by then, Jadwiga had reached twelve years, the minimum age prescribed by <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">canon law</a> for girls to marry,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHalecki199193_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalecki199193-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> she must have been born before 18 February 1374.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESroka199954–55_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESroka199954–55-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
